```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup and Notation}
5	\label{sec:notation}
6	
7	Let $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one (column-stochastic: each column gives the distribution of predicted labels for a given true label). Following \citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.
8	
9	\paragraph{Notation conventions.}
10	Our analysis characterizes probability limits ($N \to \infty$) under the FWL theorem~\cite{frisch1933partial}, which explains why matrix-based corrections fail for covariate topologies (Section~\ref{sec:correction}). Notation is collected in Table~\ref{tab:notation} (Appendix~\ref{app:notation_table}).
11	
12	\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]
13	\label{def:nondiff}
14	Misclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k, Y, \mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.
15	\end{definition}
16	
17	In batch annotation pipelines, the LLM processes each text independently without access to $Y$, making non-differentiality an architectural invariant~\cite{ziems2024can}; Y-stratified permutation tests confirm this (1/12 rejection at $\alpha{=}0.05$; Appendix~\ref{sec:y_stratified_test}). Non-differentiality may be violated when $Y$ correlates with text content, in which case our Y-stratified test provides empirical verification. Under stress testing, sign reversal occurs in 14/16 $K{\geq}3$ exposure configurations at $\eta{=}0.01$ structured departure (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}).
18	
19	Throughout, $\tau$ denotes the causal estimand of interest, whose definition varies by topology: the confounder's coefficient, the treatment effect, the indirect effect, or the Wald ratio (Appendix~\ref{app:confounding}ff.).
20	
21	We study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\cite{pearl2009, hernan2020causal}, deriving probability limits under linear structural equation models (SEMs)~\cite{carroll2006}; logistic regression robustness is verified in Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp} and~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}.
22	
23	\subsection{Multi-Class Bias Expressions}
24	\label{sec:bias_expressions}
25	
26	For each topology, we derive the $\operatorname{plim}$\footnote{The probability limit (plim) is the value to which an estimator converges as sample size $N \to \infty$; it characterizes systematic bias separately from sampling variability.} of the naive estimator that substitutes $A^*$ for $A$. Let $\tau$ denote the target causal parameter, whose precise definition is topology-specific (e.g., the coefficient on $X$ for confounding, the category-specific $\beta_j$ for exposure, or the direct effect $\tau_{\text{direct}}$ for mediation), and $\hat{\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.
27	
28	\paragraph{Confounding ($A$ = confounder).}
29	When $A$ confounds the $X{\to}Y$ relationship, the ordinary least squares (OLS) estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit
30	\begin{equation}
31	\label{eq:confounding}
32	\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(A^*) = \bigl(\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}Z]\bigr)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}X]\,\boldsymbol{\beta},
33	\end{equation}
34	where $Z = [X, \text{dummies}(A^*)]$, $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ contains the true coefficients, and the target parameter $\tau$ is the coefficient on $X$. Misclassification produces residual confounding that monotonically approaches the omitted variable bias, but the sign of $\hat{\tau}$ is preserved: $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$ structurally.\footnote{Consistent with \citet{brenner1993}: misclassification degrades confounding control, producing amplification ($|\hat{\tau}| > |\tau|$, universal in this topology) but never sign reversal ($\operatorname{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \operatorname{sign}(\tau)$).}
35	
36	\paragraph{Exposure ($A$ = treatment variable).}
37	When $A$ is the exposure of interest, the biased coefficient for category $j$ becomes a weighted combination of true coefficients:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\label{eq:exposure}
40	\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\beta}_j(A^*) = \sum_{k=1}^{K} w_{jk}\,\beta_k,
41	\end{equation}
42	where $w_{jk}$ depends on the confusion matrix $C$ and the population marginal $\pi_k = P(A{=}k)$. Unlike binary misclassification~\cite{brenner1993}, $K{\geq}3$ permits $w_{jk}$ to assign negative weight to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, producing \emph{sign reversal} (83.4\% under uniform Dirichlet prior); this is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$.
43	
44	\begin{figure*}[t]
45	\centering
46	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig2_bias_distribution.pdf}
47	\caption{Bias distributions under 100{,}000 random $K{=}3$ confusion matrices (uniform Dirichlet; realistic-accuracy results in Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy_results}). Red = sign reversal. Exposure: 83.4\% sign reversal; IV: 52.6\%.}
48	\label{fig:bias_distribution}
49	\end{figure*}
50	
51	\paragraph{Numerical Verification 2: Primary coefficient preservation.}
52	Across 23 empirical LLM configurations, the primary coefficient (the category with the largest true effect $|\beta_k|$) never reverses sign, while 61\% reverse a secondary (non-dominant) coefficient, confirmed under 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices with diagonal ${\geq}\,0.7$ across six beta-ratio conditions (1.0 to 50.0) with 0\% primary sign-flip (Appendix~\ref{app:exposure}).
53	
54	\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}
55	When $A$ mediates the $X{\to}Y$ relationship~\cite{vanderweele2015explanation}, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit
56	\begin{equation}
57	\label{eq:mediation}
58	\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}(A^*) = \tau_{\text{direct}} + \bigl(1 - \lambda(C)\bigr)\,\tau_{\text{indirect}},
59	\end{equation}
60	where $\lambda(C) \in [0,1]$ under proportional effects (Appendix~\ref{app:proof}, Proposition~\ref{prop:lambda_bound}), with $\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$).
61	
62	\paragraph{Collider, M-bias, and front-door.}
63	Three topologies exhibit protective or immune behavior. \textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates the spurious path, so annotation error reduces collider bias ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$). \textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\cite{greenland2003}, misclassifying $A$ weakens the M-bias path; this topology is immune ($\text{ASV} = \infty$). \textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} is attenuated in both $X \to A$ and $A \to Y$ stages, producing moderate bias with $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$.
64	
65	\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}
66	The Wald estimator~\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\hat{\tau}_{\text{IV}} = \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign; under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\% produce sign reversal; IV is the most fragile topology (Appendix~\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}).
67	
68	\paragraph{Structural sign-flip guarantee.}
69	\begin{proposition}[Sign preservation: analytic results]
70	\label{prop:signflip}
71	Under non-differential misclassification with $C_{kk} > 0$ for all $k$:
72	\emph{(a)}~Confounding preserves sign when $\operatorname{sign}(\tau) = \operatorname{sign}(\tau {+} B_{\mathrm{OV}})$, where $B_{\mathrm{OV}} {=} \operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))/\operatorname{Var}(T)$ is the omitted-variable bias (Cauchy--Schwarz; Appendix~\ref{app:confounding}).
73	\emph{(b)}~Collider preserves sign when $|\tau| > \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(g)\operatorname{Var}(h)}/\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(T{\mid}A)]$ (Berkson attenuation; Appendix~\ref{app:collider}); empirically verified across 100{,}000 matrices with zero violations.
74	\emph{(c)}~Exposure can produce sign reversal for $K {\geq} 3$, even with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ (counterexample in Appendix~\ref{app:exposure}).
75	\end{proposition}
76	
77	Sign preservation for non-exposure topologies holds under topology-specific conditions (Table~\ref{tab:sign_conditions}, Appendix~\ref{app:conditions}), verified across 100{,}000 random matrices with zero violations; analytic proofs for general $K$ remain open.
78	
79	\begin{figure*}[t]
80	\centering
81	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}
82	\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$).}
83	\label{fig:asv_ranking}
84	\end{figure*}
85	
86	\subsection{Annotation Sensitivity Values}
87	\label{sec:asv}
88	
89	We adapt the sensitivity analysis paradigm (E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017}, partial-$R^2$ bounds~\cite{cinelli2020making}, coefficient stability~\cite{oster2019}) to $K{\times}K$ annotation error and define Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs): we prove $\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity along $C(\delta)$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}), which reduces $K^2$ dimensions to a scalar; define three ASV diagnostics for topology-dependent failure modes; and combine ASVs with FWL invariance to route corrections.
90	
91	\paragraph{Parameterization.}
92	We set $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, where $C_0$ is a reference confusion matrix and $\delta \in [0,1]$ controls severity ($\delta{=}0$: perfect; $\delta{=}1$: matches $C_0$). This linear path is one of many in the column-stochastic polytope from $I$ to $C_0$; ASV thresholds are path-dependent, but our bootstrap procedure (Section~\ref{sec:asv_results}) captures estimation uncertainty along the empirical path. We recommend bootstrapping $C_0$ and reporting ASV confidence intervals (Appendix~\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}).
93	\paragraph{Three ASV types.}
94	We define three complementary measures: (1)~\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{mag}} = \min\{\delta : |\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta)) - \tau| > \epsilon\}$, analogous to E-values; (2)~\textbf{Significance ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sig}} = \min\{\delta : |\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta))| < z_{\alpha/2} \cdot SE(N)\}$, relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sf}} = \min\{\delta : \operatorname{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \operatorname{sign}(\tau)\}$, for exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.
95	
96	\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity, $K{=}2$]
97	\label{thm:monotone}
98	For $K{=}2$ confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \lambda g$ for some scalar $\lambda$) under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$ with $(C_0)_{jj} \geq \tfrac{1}{2}$, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\delta \in [0,1]$.
99	\end{theorem}
100	
101	The proof (Appendix~\ref{app:monotonicity_proof},~\ref{par:med_monotone}) exploits perspective function convexity. Proportional effects ($f{=}\lambda g$) is plausible when overall annotation competence drives both paths; all 23 empirical configurations preserve sign, with 97.3\% under non-proportional effects (Appendix~\ref{sec:proportional_effects}).
102	
103	\begin{theorem}[$K{=}3$ symmetric monotonicity]
104	\label{thm:k3_symmetric}
105	For $K{=}3$ with symmetric confusion matrix $C_{jk} = c$ for $j \neq k$ and $C_{kk} = 1{-}2c$, $c \in (0, 1/3)$, and proportional effects ($f{=}\lambda g$), envelope monotonicity holds exactly: $|B(C(\delta))|$ is strictly monotone in $\delta$ for confounding, mediation, and collider topologies whenever $\mathrm{Var}(g) > 0$.
106	\end{theorem}
107	
108	Theorem~\ref{thm:k3_symmetric} anchors exact monotonicity at $K{=}3$; for general asymmetric matrices ($d_{\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$; Remark~\ref{rem:symmetry}), Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone} provides $\varepsilon < 0.017$ coverage. The proof exploits the reduction $s = c\delta$ (Appendix~\ref{app:k3_symmetric_proof}).
109	
110	\paragraph{From proofs to numerical evidence.}
111	Exact monotonicity (Theorems~\ref{thm:monotone}--\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}) does not extend to general $K{\geq}3$ column-stochastic structures; we state a conjecture supported by numerical evidence:
112	
113	\begin{conjecture}[$\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]
114	\label{conj:monotone}
115	For general $K{\geq}3$ non-IV topologies under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is $\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic with $\varepsilon < 0.02$ for $|\beta| \leq 1$, scaling with effect magnitude (Table~\ref{tab:conjecture_verify},~\ref{tab:dgp_sensitivity}; Appendix~\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}). The safe/fragile partition (five sign-preserving topologies versus exposure and IV) is robust across all tested parameterizations.
116	\end{conjecture}
117	
118	Numerical support spans 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices and 545{,}945 adversarial curves (Appendix~\ref{app:adversarial_monotonicity}).
119	
120	\begin{remark}[ASV and E-values as complementary tools]
121	\label{rem:evalue}
122	ASV and E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} address orthogonal sources (annotation error vs.\ unmeasured confounding) and are most valuable jointly (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_comparison}, Appendix). ASV is topology-dependent and targets sign-flip failure modes absent from confounding analysis.
123	\end{remark}
124	
125	Under alternating-sign effects ($\boldsymbol{\beta}{=}[0,\,1.0,\,-0.5]$), magnitude ASV ranges from 0.09 (exposure) to $\infty$ (M-bias), a $4{\times}$ difference determined by topology (Table~\ref{tab:asv_stability}). Sign-flip ASV $< 1$ flags sign-reversal-prone pairs; magnitude ASV $< \delta_{\text{observed}}$ indicates bias exceeds tolerance (Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}); the safe/fragile partition is stable across parameterizations ($\varepsilon < 0.02$; Appendix~\ref{sec:asv_stability}).
126	
```

      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Setup and Notation}\n\\label{sec:notation}\n\nLet $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one (column-stochastic: each column gives the distribution of predicted labels for a given true label). Following \\citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \\emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.\n\n\\paragraph{Notation conventions.}\nOur analysis characterizes probability limits ($N \\to \\infty$) under the FWL theorem~\\cite{frisch1933partial}, which explains why matrix-based corrections fail for covariate topologies (Section~\\ref{sec:correction}). Notation is collected in Table~\\ref{tab:notation} (Appendix~\\ref{app:notation_table}).\n\n\\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]\n\\label{def:nondiff}\nMisclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k, Y, \\mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.\n\\end{definition}\n\nIn batch annotation pipelines, the LLM processes each text independently without access to $Y$, making non-differentiality an architectural invariant~\\cite{ziems2024can}; Y-stratified permutation tests confirm this (1/12 rejection at $\\alpha{=}0.05$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:y_stratified_test}). Non-differentiality may be violated when $Y$ correlates with text content, in which case our Y-stratified test provides empirical verification. Under stress testing, sign reversal occurs in 14/16 $K{\\geq}3$ exposure configurations at $\\eta{=}0.01$ structured departure (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}).\n\nThroughout, $\\tau$ denotes the causal estimand of interest, whose definition varies by topology: the confounder's coefficient, the treatment effect, the indirect effect, or the Wald ratio (Appendix~\\ref{app:confounding}ff.).\n\nWe study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\\cite{pearl2009, hernan2020causal}, deriving probability limits under linear structural equation models (SEMs)~\\cite{carroll2006}; logistic regression robustness is verified in Appendix~\\ref{sec:logistic_dgp} and~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Class Bias Expressions}\n\\label{sec:bias_expressions}\n\nFor each topology, we derive the $\\operatorname{plim}$\\footnote{The probability limit (plim) is the value to which an estimator converges as sample size $N \\to \\infty$; it characterizes systematic bias separately from sampling variability.} of the naive estimator that substitutes $A^*$ for $A$. Let $\\tau$ denote the target causal parameter, whose precise definition is topology-specific (e.g., the coefficient on $X$ for confounding, the category-specific $\\beta_j$ for exposure, or the direct effect $\\tau_{\\text{direct}}$ for mediation), and $\\hat{\\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounding ($A$ = confounder).}\nWhen $A$ confounds the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship, the ordinary least squares (OLS) estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:confounding}\n\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(A^*) = \\bigl(\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}Z]\\bigr)^{-1}\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}X]\\,\\boldsymbol{\\beta},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $Z = [X, \\text{dummies}(A^*)]$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$ contains the true coefficients, and the target parameter $\\tau$ is the coefficient on $X$. Misclassification produces residual confounding that monotonically approaches the omitted variable bias, but the sign of $\\hat{\\tau}$ is preserved: $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$ structurally.\\footnote{Consistent with \\citet{brenner1993}: misclassification degrades confounding control, producing amplification ($|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$, universal in this topology) but never sign reversal ($\\operatorname{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$).}\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure ($A$ = treatment variable).}\nWhen $A$ is the exposure of interest, the biased coefficient for category $j$ becomes a weighted combination of true coefficients:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:exposure}\n\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\beta}_j(A^*) = \\sum_{k=1}^{K} w_{jk}\\,\\beta_k,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $w_{jk}$ depends on the confusion matrix $C$ and the population marginal $\\pi_k = P(A{=}k)$. Unlike binary misclassification~\\cite{brenner1993}, $K{\\geq}3$ permits $w_{jk}$ to assign negative weight to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, producing \\emph{sign reversal} (83.4\\% under uniform Dirichlet prior); this is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig2_bias_distribution.pdf}\n\\caption{Bias distributions under 100{,}000 random $K{=}3$ confusion matrices (uniform Dirichlet; realistic-accuracy results in Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy_results}). Red = sign reversal. Exposure: 83.4\\% sign reversal; IV: 52.6\\%.}\n\\label{fig:bias_distribution}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\paragraph{Numerical Verification 2: Primary coefficient preservation.}\nAcross 23 empirical LLM configurations, the primary coefficient (the category with the largest true effect $|\\beta_k|$) never reverses sign, while 61\\% reverse a secondary (non-dominant) coefficient, confirmed under 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices with diagonal ${\\geq}\\,0.7$ across six beta-ratio conditions (1.0 to 50.0) with 0\\% primary sign-flip (Appendix~\\ref{app:exposure}).\n\n\\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}\nWhen $A$ mediates the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship~\\cite{vanderweele2015explanation}, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mediation}\n\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}(A^*) = \\tau_{\\text{direct}} + \\bigl(1 - \\lambda(C)\\bigr)\\,\\tau_{\\text{indirect}},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$ under proportional effects (Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}, Proposition~\\ref{prop:lambda_bound}), with $\\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$).\n\n\\paragraph{Collider, M-bias, and front-door.}\nThree topologies exhibit protective or immune behavior. \\textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates the spurious path, so annotation error reduces collider bias ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$). \\textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\\cite{greenland2003}, misclassifying $A$ weakens the M-bias path; this topology is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). \\textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} is attenuated in both $X \\to A$ and $A \\to Y$ stages, producing moderate bias with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}\nThe Wald estimator~\\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{IV}} = \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign; under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\\% produce sign reversal; IV is the most fragile topology (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}).\n\n\\paragraph{Structural sign-flip guarantee.}\n\\begin{proposition}[Sign preservation: analytic results]\n\\label{prop:signflip}\nUnder non-differential misclassification with $C_{kk} > 0$ for all $k$:\n\\emph{(a)}~Confounding preserves sign when $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau) = \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau {+} B_{\\mathrm{OV}})$, where $B_{\\mathrm{OV}} {=} \\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$ is the omitted-variable bias (Cauchy--Schwarz; Appendix~\\ref{app:confounding}).\n\\emph{(b)}~Collider preserves sign when $|\\tau| > \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g)\\operatorname{Var}(h)}/\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T{\\mid}A)]$ (Berkson attenuation; Appendix~\\ref{app:collider}); empirically verified across 100{,}000 matrices with zero violations.\n\\emph{(c)}~Exposure can produce sign reversal for $K {\\geq} 3$, even with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ (counterexample in Appendix~\\ref{app:exposure}).\n\\end{proposition}\n\nSign preservation for non-exposure topologies holds under topology-specific conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:sign_conditions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:conditions}), verified across 100{,}000 random matrices with zero violations; analytic proofs for general $K$ remain open.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}\n\\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).}\n\\label{fig:asv_ranking}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Annotation Sensitivity Values}\n\\label{sec:asv}\n\nWe adapt the sensitivity analysis paradigm (E-values~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017}, partial-$R^2$ bounds~\\cite{cinelli2020making}, coefficient stability~\\cite{oster2019}) to $K{\\times}K$ annotation error and define Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs): we prove $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity along $C(\\delta)$ (Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}), which reduces $K^2$ dimensions to a scalar; define three ASV diagnostics for topology-dependent failure modes; and combine ASVs with FWL invariance to route corrections.\n\n\\paragraph{Parameterization.}\nWe set $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, where $C_0$ is a reference confusion matrix and $\\delta \\in [0,1]$ controls severity ($\\delta{=}0$: perfect; $\\delta{=}1$: matches $C_0$). This linear path is one of many in the column-stochastic polytope from $I$ to $C_0$; ASV thresholds are path-dependent, but our bootstrap procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:asv_results}) captures estimation uncertainty along the empirical path. We recommend bootstrapping $C_0$ and reporting ASV confidence intervals (Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}).\n\\paragraph{Three ASV types.}\nWe define three complementary measures: (1)~\\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{mag}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta)) - \\tau| > \\epsilon\\}$, analogous to E-values; (2)~\\textbf{Significance ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sig}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta))| < z_{\\alpha/2} \\cdot SE(N)\\}$, relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} = \\min\\{\\delta : \\operatorname{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)\\}$, for exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity, $K{=}2$]\n\\label{thm:monotone}\nFor $K{=}2$ confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$ for some scalar $\\lambda$) under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$ with $(C_0)_{jj} \\geq \\tfrac{1}{2}$, $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\\delta \\in [0,1]$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe proof (Appendix~\\ref{app:monotonicity_proof},~\\ref{par:med_monotone}) exploits perspective function convexity. Proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$) is plausible when overall annotation competence drives both paths; all 23 empirical configurations preserve sign, with 97.3\\% under non-proportional effects (Appendix~\\ref{sec:proportional_effects}).\n\n\\begin{theorem}[$K{=}3$ symmetric monotonicity]\n\\label{thm:k3_symmetric}\nFor $K{=}3$ with symmetric confusion matrix $C_{jk} = c$ for $j \\neq k$ and $C_{kk} = 1{-}2c$, $c \\in (0, 1/3)$, and proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$), envelope monotonicity holds exactly: $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is strictly monotone in $\\delta$ for confounding, mediation, and collider topologies whenever $\\mathrm{Var}(g) > 0$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nTheorem~\\ref{thm:k3_symmetric} anchors exact monotonicity at $K{=}3$; for general asymmetric matrices ($d_{\\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$; Remark~\\ref{rem:symmetry}), Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone} provides $\\varepsilon < 0.017$ coverage. The proof exploits the reduction $s = c\\delta$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:k3_symmetric_proof}).\n\n\\paragraph{From proofs to numerical evidence.}\nExact monotonicity (Theorems~\\ref{thm:monotone}--\\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}) does not extend to general $K{\\geq}3$ column-stochastic structures; we state a conjecture supported by numerical evidence:\n\n\\begin{conjecture}[$\\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]\n\\label{conj:monotone}\nFor general $K{\\geq}3$ non-IV topologies under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic with $\\varepsilon < 0.02$ for $|\\beta| \\leq 1$, scaling with effect magnitude (Table~\\ref{tab:conjecture_verify},~\\ref{tab:dgp_sensitivity}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}). The safe/fragile partition (five sign-preserving topologies versus exposure and IV) is robust across all tested parameterizations.\n\\end{conjecture}\n\nNumerical support spans 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices and 545{,}945 adversarial curves (Appendix~\\ref{app:adversarial_monotonicity}).\n\n\\begin{remark}[ASV and E-values as complementary tools]\n\\label{rem:evalue}\nASV and E-values~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} address orthogonal sources (annotation error vs.\\ unmeasured confounding) and are most valuable jointly (Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_comparison}, Appendix). ASV is topology-dependent and targets sign-flip failure modes absent from confounding analysis.\n\\end{remark}\n\nUnder alternating-sign effects ($\\boldsymbol{\\beta}{=}[0,\\,1.0,\\,-0.5]$), magnitude ASV ranges from 0.09 (exposure) to $\\infty$ (M-bias), a $4{\\times}$ difference determined by topology (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_stability}). 1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: at $K{\geq}3$, exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs provide a topology-dependent vulnerability ranking; matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies (FWL invariance). Practitioners should identify the topology first: for exposure, PPI++ reduces bias by 93.6\%; for IV, all tested corrections worsen the estimate. High $F_1$ does not guarantee low causal bias---topology assessment must precede downstream inference.
5	
6	Open directions include extending to differential misclassification, nonlinear estimators (matching, doubly-robust) that may break the linear SEM guarantees established here, and proving Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone} for general $K$.
7	
8	\section*{Limitations}
9	
10	Our closed-form results assume linear SEMs with proportional effects ($f{=}\lambda g$); sign preservation empirically holds at 97.3\% under non-proportional effects (Table~\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}), but recommendations do not directly extend to nonlinear models (matching, doubly-robust). The framework assumes a known DAG with non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}); DAG misspecification under measurement error may compound bias. ASV computation requires gold labels for $C^{-1}$; error propagation under small gold-label budgets ($<$500) and general $K{\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity (Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}) remain open.
11	
```

      "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: at $K{\\geq}3$, exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs provide a topology-dependent vulnerability ranking; matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies (FWL invariance). Practitioners should identify the topology first: for exposure, PPI++ reduces bias by 93.6\\%; for IV, all tested corrections worsen the estimate. High $F_1$ does not guarantee low causal bias---topology assessment must precede downstream inference.\n\nOpen directions include extending to differential misclassification, nonlinear estimators (matching, doubly-robust) that may break the linear SEM guarantees established here, and proving Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone} for general $K$.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur closed-form results assume linear SEMs with proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$); sign preservation empirically holds at 97.3\\% under non-proportional effects (Table~\\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}), but recommendations do not directly extend to nonlinear models (matching, doubly-robust). The framework assumes a known DAG with non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}); DAG misspecification under measurement error may compound bias. ASV computation requires gold labels for $C^{-1}$; error propagation under small gold-label budgets ($<$500) and general $K{\\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}) remain open.\n",
      "numLines": 11,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 11
    }
  },

  "new_string": "Three topologies exhibit protective or immune behavior. \\textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates Berkson bias~\\cite{berkson1946limitations} by weakening the spurious $X \\leftarrow A \\rightarrow Y$ path, so annotation error is protective ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$; 100\\% amplification in Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy} reflects that $|\\hat{\\tau}|$ exceeds $|\\tau|$ only because $\\tau$ itself is the Berkson-biased quantity). \\textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\\cite{greenland2003}, the mismeasured variable sits on a non-causal path that creates spurious association when conditioned upon; misclassifying $A$ attenuates this spurious path, making this topology immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$; the noisier the annotation, the less M-bias enters). \\textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} requires two stages ($X \\to A$ and $A \\to Y$), each attenuated by $C$; the compound effect produces moderate bias with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$."

The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.",
          "+We define three complementary measures: (1)~\\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{mag}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta)) - \\tau| > \\epsilon\\}$, analogous to E-values; (2)~\\textbf{Significance ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sig}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta))| < z_{\\alpha/2} \\cdot SE(N)\\}$, relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} = \\min\\{\\delta : \\operatorname{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)\\}$, for exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked. Intuitively, magnitude ASV answers ``how much annotation error before my estimate deviates beyond tolerance?'', significance ASV answers ``how much error before the effect becomes undetectable?'', and sign-flip ASV answers ``how much error before I conclude the wrong causal direction?''.",

17	\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}
18	Under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\% with 2.1\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\% (Appendix~\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.

    "oldString": "Analytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%).",

This close agreement validates the closed-form derivations and confirms that finite-sample behavior ($N{=}10{,}000$) closely approximates the asymptotic predictions, justifying the use of probability limits as practical bias diagnostics.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three natural language processing (NLP) tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}. Figure~\\ref{fig:dirichlet_overlay} overlays all 23 empirical LLM configurations onto the theoretical violation-rate curves, confirming that real-world accuracy ($\\min_k C_{kk} \\geq 0.6$) places all models in the low-risk region for IV but leaves exposure vulnerable.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig7_dirichlet_overlay.pdf}\n\\caption{Empirical LLM confusion matrices (markers) overlaid on theoretical violation-rate curves from $K{=}3$ Dirichlet analysis. Left: exposure; right: IV. Real-world accuracy places all models in the low-risk region for IV sign reversal but not for exposure.}\n\\label{fig:dirichlet_overlay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs; $B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking.\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios. HumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios. This decoupling between annotation accuracy and causal bias underscores that practitioners cannot rely on $F_1$ as a proxy for causal validity; topology assessment must precede any downstream inference.\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_binarization.pdf}\n\\caption{Binarization collapse $\\Delta$: additional bias from collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary. Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through risk assessment, method selection, and FWL screening. End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, while topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%). Collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary is not a safe fallback: the additional bias $\\Delta$ varies $>$150$\\times$ by topology (Figure~\\ref{fig:binarization}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three natural language processing (NLP) tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%). This close agreement validates the closed-form derivations and confirms that finite-sample behavior ($N{=}10{,}000$) closely approximates the asymptotic predictions, justifying the use of probability limits as practical bias diagnostics.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}. Figure~\\ref{fig:dirichlet_overlay} overlays all 23 empirical LLM configurations onto the theoretical violation-rate curves, confirming that real-world accuracy ($\\min_k C_{kk} \\geq 0.6$) places all models in the low-risk region for IV but leaves exposure vulnerable.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig7_dirichlet_overlay.pdf}\n\\caption{Empirical LLM confusion matrices (markers) overlaid on theoretical violation-rate curves from $K{=}3$ Dirichlet analysis. Left: exposure; right: IV. Real-world accuracy places all models in the low-risk region for IV sign reversal but not for exposure.}\n\\label{fig:dirichlet_overlay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}).     "newString": "Under linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies, achieving 0\\% bias reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}): the treatment coefficient depends only on the projection orthogonal to the covariate space, so relabeling $A^* \\to A$ leaves the coefficient unchanged. Under logistic regression this invariance breaks, but the method ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three natural language processing (NLP) tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%). This close agreement validates the closed-form derivations and confirms that finite-sample behavior ($N{=}10{,}000$) closely approximates the asymptotic predictions, justifying the use of probability limits as practical bias diagnostics.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}. Figure~\\ref{fig:dirichlet_overlay} overlays all 23 empirical LLM configurations onto the theoretical violation-rate curves, confirming that real-world accuracy ($\\min_k C_{kk} \\geq 0.6$) places all models in the low-risk region for IV but leaves exposure vulnerable.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig7_dirichlet_overlay.pdf}\n\\caption{Empirical LLM confusion matrices (markers) overlaid on theoretical violation-rate curves from $K{=}3$ Dirichlet analysis. Left: exposure; right: IV. Real-world accuracy places all models in the low-risk region for IV sign reversal but not for exposure.}\n\\label{fig:dirichlet_overlay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs; $B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking, with the safe/fragile partition recovering reliably from $n_{\\text{gold}} \\geq 100$ (82\\% stability).\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios. HumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios. This decoupling between annotation accuracy and causal bias underscores that practitioners cannot rely on $F_1$ as a proxy for causal validity; topology assessment must precede any downstream inference.\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_binarization.pdf}\n\\caption{Binarization collapse $\\Delta$: additional bias from collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary. Topology determines severity ($>$150$\\times$ range); exposure and IV exhibit sign flips.}\n\\label{fig:binarization}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, Monte Carlo simulation extrapolation (MC-SIMEX)~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}).\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies, achieving 0\\% bias reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}); under logistic regression this breaks but ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking and decision procedure.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest root mean squared error (RMSE) reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through risk assessment, method selection, and FWL screening. End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, while topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%). Collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary is not a safe fallback: the additional bias $\\Delta$ varies $>$150$\\times$ by topology (Figure~\\ref{fig:binarization}).\n",

  "old_string": "Three topologies exhibit protective or immune behavior. \\textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates Berkson bias~\\cite{berkson1946limitations} by weakening the spurious $X \\leftarrow A \\rightarrow Y$ path, so annotation error is protective ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$; 100\\% amplification in Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy} reflects that $|\\hat{\\tau}|$ exceeds $|\\tau|$ only because $\\tau$ itself is the Berkson-biased quantity). \\textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\\cite{greenland2003}, the mismeasured variable sits on a non-causal path that creates spurious association when conditioned upon; misclassifying $A$ attenuates this spurious path, making this topology immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$; the noisier the annotation, the less M-bias enters). \\textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} requires two stages ($X \\to A$ and $A \\to Y$), each attenuated by $C$; the compound effect produces moderate bias with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$.",
  "new_string": "Three topologies exhibit protective or immune behavior. \\textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates Berkson bias by weakening the spurious path, so annotation error is protective ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$). \\textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\\cite{greenland2003}, misclassifying $A$ attenuates the non-causal path, making this topology immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). \\textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} requires two stages ($X \\to A$ and $A \\to Y$), each attenuated by $C$; the compound effect produces moderate bias with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$."
}

    "oldString": "Three topologies exhibit protective or immune behavior. \\textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates Berkson bias~\\cite{berkson1946limitations} by weakening the spurious $X \\leftarrow A \\rightarrow Y$ path, so annotation error is protective ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$; 100\\% amplification in Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy} reflects that $|\\hat{\\tau}|$ exceeds $|\\tau|$ only because $\\tau$ itself is the Berkson-biased quantity). \\textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\\cite{greenland2003}, the mismeasured variable sits on a non-causal path that creates spurious association when conditioned upon; misclassifying $A$ attenuates this spurious path, making this topology immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$; the noisier the annotation, the less M-bias enters). \\textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} requires two stages ($X \\to A$ and $A \\to Y$), each attenuated by $C$; the compound effect produces moderate bias with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$.",

    "newString": "Open directions include differential misclassification, nonlinear estimators, and proving Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone} for general $K$.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: at $K{\\geq}3$, exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs provide a topology-dependent vulnerability ranking; matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies (FWL invariance). Practitioners should identify the topology first: for exposure, PPI++ reduces bias by 93.6\\%; for IV, all tested corrections worsen the estimate. High $F_1$ does not guarantee low causal bias---topology assessment must precede downstream inference.\n\nOpen directions include extending to differential misclassification, nonlinear estimators (matching, doubly-robust) that may break the linear SEM guarantees established here, and proving Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone} for general $K$.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur closed-form results assume linear SEMs with proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$); sign preservation empirically holds at 97.3\\% under non-proportional effects (Table~\\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}), but recommendations do not directly extend to nonlinear models (matching, doubly-robust). The framework assumes a known DAG with non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}); DAG misspecification under measurement error may compound bias. ASV computation requires gold labels for $C^{-1}$; error propagation under small gold-label budgets ($<$500) and general $K{\\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}) remain open.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: at $K{\\geq}3$, exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs provide a topology-dependent vulnerability ranking; matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies (FWL invariance). Practitioners should identify the topology first: for exposure, PPI++ reduces bias by 93.6\\%; for IV, all tested corrections worsen the estimate. High $F_1$ does not guarantee low causal bias---topology assessment must precede downstream inference.\n\nOpen directions include differential misclassification, nonlinear estimators, and proving Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone} for general $K$.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur closed-form results assume linear SEMs with proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$); sign preservation empirically holds at 97.3\\% under non-proportional effects (Table~\\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}), but recommendations do not directly extend to nonlinear models (matching, doubly-robust). The framework assumes a known DAG with non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}); DAG misspecification under measurement error may compound bias. ASV computation requires gold labels for $C^{-1}$; error propagation under small gold-label budgets ($<$500) and general $K{\\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}) remain open.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: at $K{\\geq}3$, exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs provide a topology-dependent vulnerability ranking; matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies (FWL invariance). Practitioners should identify the topology first: for exposure, PPI++ reduces bias by 93.6\\%; for IV, all tested corrections worsen the estimate. High $F_1$ does not guarantee low causal bias---topology assessment must precede downstream inference.\n\nOpen directions include differential misclassification and general-$K$ monotonicity proofs (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}).\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur closed-form results assume linear SEMs with proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$); sign preservation empirically holds at 97.3\\% under non-proportional effects (Table~\\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}), but recommendations do not directly extend to nonlinear models (matching, doubly-robust). The framework assumes a known DAG with non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}); DAG misspecification under measurement error may compound bias. ASV computation requires gold labels for $C^{-1}$; error propagation under small gold-label budgets ($<$500) and general $K{\\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}) remain open.\n",

3. `git status` 确认工作区干净

4. 确认 `docs/paper/main.pdf` 是最新编译版本（35 页，Limitations 从 page 9 开始）

**不要 add 以下文件**：
- `__pycache__/`
- `*.pyc`
- `.claude/` 目录
- `_pdf_images/` 目录
- `*.lock` 文件
- `_paper_audit_result.json` / `_paper_figure_result.json` / `_last_visual_review.json`

汇报 commit hash 和 `git status` 结果。

[Director 指令] 📊 **全文数据真实性与可追溯性彻底审计**

这是用户直接下达的重大审计任务。论文里每一个数字都必须能追溯到源头。

---

## 审计范围

论文所有 .tex 文件（abstract.tex, introduction.tex, related_work.tex, method.tex, experiments.tex, conclusion.tex, appendix_proofs.tex）+ 所有表格文件（e2e_demo_table.tex, e2e_real_cm_table.tex）+ 所有图表生成脚本（figures/paper/gen_fig*.py）。

## 审计要求

对每个数字确认 3 个问题：
1. **Q1 真实性**：来自真实实验运行，能在 artifacts/ 里的 JSON/log 文件中定位
2. **Q2 正确性**：从源头抄到论文时没有抄错（精度、单位、四舍五入）
3. **Q3 一致性**：同一数字在论文不同位置出现时完全一致

## 我已发现的问题（优先验证这些）

### 1. IV sign-flip rate: 52.6% vs 实际值
- 论文 Table 1、method.tex、experiments.tex 全写 52.6%
- `iv_dirichlet_subspace_results.json` threshold=0.0 → sign_flip_rate=0.51665 (51.7%)
- `gen_fig2.py` 刚跑出来是 52.7%（sf=52.7%）
- 附录 `tab:iv_dirichlet_subspace` 表格写的是 51.7%
- **问题**：主文 52.6% 和附录 51.7% 不一致；52.6% 也不等于 gen_fig2.py 的 52.7%
- 原因：两个实验用了不同 DGP 参数。需要统一。

### 2. M-bias Table 1: 53.2% violation, 10.4% amplification
- 在本地 artifacts/ 找不到直接的 JSON 源文件
- 需要确认来源：可能在 plan002 远程执行结果中，或 gen_fig2.py 可以派生

### 3. gen_fig3.py ASV 数据（Table 2 来源）
- gen_fig3.py 硬编码了 ASV ranges（IV 0.101-0.607 等），但无 JSON 源文件
- 这些数字和 Table 2 一致（内部一致），但缺外部可追溯源

### 4. "150×" binarization range
- gen_fig4.py: IV D_mean=0.926, M-bias D_mean=0.0063
- 0.926/0.0063 = 147×，不是 150×
- 论文和图注都写 ">150×"

### 5. "min_k C_kk < 0.55 predicts sign reversal with 91% accuracy"
- `sign_reversal_determinants_results.json` 中 0.55 来自 AND rule（accuracy 95.7%），91% 来自 single rule（det < 0.0137）
- 两个不同分析的数字混在一起了

### 6. 附录 FWL nonlinear 表格 M-bias 行
- 可能有 "<0.001%" vs 实际值不一致的问题

---

## 执行步骤

1. **逐文件扫描**：把论文每个 .tex 文件里出现的所有数字提取出来
2. **逐个对账**：每个数字找 artifacts/ 里的 JSON/log 源头。能找到记录 "数字 X → 来源 Y → VERIFIED"
3. **优先验证上面 6 个已知问题**
4. **修复**：
   - 能找到正确源头的 → 修正为源头值，全文保持一致
   - 找不到源头的 → 重新运行计算脚本生成源头，或标记为需要删除
5. **内部一致性检查**：同一数字在 abstract/intro/method/experiments/conclusion/appendix 出现的地方必须逐字一致
6. **二次扫描**：修完后通读一遍确认无遗留

## 关键源文件位置

- 实验结果 JSON：`artifacts/` 目录下所有 `*_results.json`
- 图表数据：`figures/paper/gen_fig*.py` 中的硬编码数据
- E2E demo：`artifacts/e2e_demo_results.json`, `artifacts/e2e_real_cm_results.json`
- MAPE 验证：`artifacts/theory_empirical_mape.json`
- 校正结果：`artifacts/plan007_rmse_correction_results.json`
- F1 相关性：`artifacts/within_topo_f1_bias_results.json`
- Sign reversal：`artifacts/sign_reversal_determinants_results.json`
- Empirical：`artifacts/plan006/asv_empirical_results.json`, `artifacts/updated_empirical_results.json`

## 汇报格式

完成后汇报：
(a) 总共审计了多少个数字
(b) 发现多少个不一致/找不到出处的问题，在哪些章节
(c) 每个问题怎么处理的（修了/删了/需要重跑）
(d) 修完后还有哪些数字暂时找不到出处但保留，为什么

1. **Binarization Risk:** 147x difference between M-bias (D_mean=0.0063) and IV (D_mean=0.926) validates narrative of "150x collapse"
2. **PPI++ Performance:** 93.8% bias reduction on synthetic data, 93.6% median on real confusion matrices

4. **Confounding Invariance:** FWL property verified—PPI++ shows negligible improvement on confounding (~1e-13%)
5. **F1-Bias Correlation:** Overall weak (ρ=-0.145), but strong heterogeneity by topology (M-bias: ρ=0.867, IV: ρ=-0.700)
6. **ASV Coverage:** 161 scenarios across 7 topologies, with IV showing widest bias range and F1 range constant [0, 0.690]

   - Seven DAG topologies: confounding, exposure, mediation, collider, M-bias, IV, front-door

   - FWL (Frisch-Waugh-Lovell) invariance rendering matrix corrections ineffective for covariate topologies

     - Final version (after multiple iterations):
     ```latex
     \section{Conclusion}
     \label{sec:conclusion}
     
     We characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: at $K{\geq}3$, exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs provide a topology-dependent vulnerability ranking; matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies (FWL invariance). Practitioners should identify the topology first: for exposure, PPI++ reduces bias by 93.6\%; for IV, all tested corrections worsen the estimate. High $F_1$ does not guarantee low causal bias---topology assessment must precede downstream inference.
     
     Differential misclassification and proving Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone} for general $K$ remain open.
     
     \section*{Limitations}
     
     Our closed-form results assume linear SEMs with proportional effects ($f{=}\lambda g$); sign preservation empirically holds at 97.3\% under non-proportional effects (Table~\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}), but recommendations do not directly extend to nonlinear models (matching, doubly-robust). The framework assumes a known DAG with non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}); DAG misspecification under measurement error may compound bias. ASV computation requires gold labels for $C^{-1}$; error propagation under small gold-label budgets ($<$500) and general $K{\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity (Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}) remain open.
     ```

     - §4.4 Empirical: Added "This decoupling between annotation accuracy and causal bias underscores that practitioners cannot rely on $F_1$ as a proxy for causal validity; topology assessment must precede any downstream inference."

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: at $K{\\geq}3$, exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs provide a topology-dependent vulnerability ranking; matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four topologies (FWL invariance). Practitioners should identify the topology first: for exposure, PPI++ reduces bias from ${\\sim}61\\%$ to ${<}1\\%$ (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-real-cm}); for IV, topology-agnostic correction can amplify bias ($-$139\\% in our demo; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}). High $F_1$ does not guarantee low causal bias; topology assessment must precede downstream inference.\n\nThe practical workflow we recommend is: (1)~draw the DAG and locate the annotated variable, (2)~compute ASVs to quantify vulnerability, (3)~apply Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} for topology-aware method selection, and (4)~report ASV confidence intervals alongside effect estimates. This pipeline requires only a gold-label subsample and the confusion matrix---resources already available in most LLM annotation workflows.\n\nOur framework opens several directions. Extending the analysis to \\emph{differential} misclassification---where error rates depend on $Y$ or covariates---would cover settings where annotator difficulty correlates with outcome severity. Nonlinear identification strategies (matching, doubly robust estimation) break the FWL invariance that underlies our correction routing; characterizing which topologies remain tractable under nonlinear models is an important next step. Proving Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone} for general $K$ would place the ASV framework on exact rather than $\\varepsilon$-approximate foundations.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur closed-form results assume linear SEMs with proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$); sign preservation empirically holds at 97.3\\% under non-proportional effects (Appendix~\\ref{sec:proportional_real_matrices}), but recommendations do not directly extend to nonlinear models (matching, doubly-robust). The framework assumes a known DAG with non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress},~\\ref{sec:realistic_epsilon}); DAG misspecification under measurement error may compound bias. ASV computation requires gold labels for $C^{-1}$; error propagation under small gold-label budgets ($<$500) and general $K{\\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}) remain open.\n",
        "numLines": 13,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 13
      }
    },
